    22	
    23	/**
    24	 * The acceptance engine — #4's matrix, entire.
    25	 *
    26	 * The reducer holds the *floor* of this matrix (`authority.ts`): the rows that
    27	 * are trust boundaries, enforced where nothing can route around them. This file
    28	 * decides what a worker should emit at all, given inputs the reducer applies
    29	 * more narrowly — the messages a reading was drawn from, who wrote them, what
    30	 * the room has already accepted.
    31	 *
    32	 * The two read one θ table (`policy.ts`), so they cannot disagree about whether
    33	 * a reading is strong enough, and `AcceptanceConfig` refuses to be configured
    34	 * below the floor, so the engine can only ever be the stricter of the two.
    35	 *
    36	 * ## What "two boundaries" does and does not mean
    37	 *
    38	 * r4's receipt said the engine and the reducer are two boundaries, unqualified,
    39	 * and r4's blind review was right that the claim outruns the code. Both call the
    40	 * *same* `validateProposalProvenance`, so **every defect in quote semantics
    41	 * survives both**: switching off the engine does not help, because the reducer
    42	 * accepts what the validator passes; switching off the reducer does not help,
    43	 * because the engine emits `auto_accept` on the same input.
    44	 *
    45	 * That is deliberate and it stays. Two implementations of "does this quote bear
    46	 * this sentence" is two answers to one question, which is the defect
    47	 * `policy.ts` exists to prevent for θ — and `RETRO.md` records what it costs
    48	 * when a rule has two homes. So the claim is scoped instead of withdrawn or
    49	 * padded:
    50	 *
    51	 *  - **Independently enforced at the floor** (`authority.ts`, and nothing here
    52	 *    can weaken them): who the actor is, that a proposal is cited and is real,
    53	 *    open, type-matching and room-matching, that a non-human acted on its own
    54	 *    reading, the confidence floor, payload equality, provenance equality, that
    55	 *    a message window exists at all, and that a quote exists at all.
    56	 *  - **One implementation, called from both**: everything about what the quote
    57	 *    *means* — where it sits in its message, what the statement drops or adds,
    58	 *    who wrote it, and whether the window revisits it. A defect here is a defect
    59	 *    in both layers, and `boundary.test.ts` pins that as a property rather than
    60	 *    letting a receipt imply otherwise.
    61	 *
    62	 * **The receipt is not optional, and an empty one is not a receipt.** In round 1
    63	 * `messages` was an optional field whose absence produced an empty problem set,
    64	 * so the caller that forgot it got auto-acceptance instead of a refusal — the
    65	 * exact fail-open shape a trust boundary must not have. Round 2 made it required
    66	 * and round 2's gauntlet walked through the door that was left: `messages: []`
    67	 * satisfies "required" and satisfies nothing else. A model proposal judged with
    68	 * no window *or an empty one* is discarded with `missing_message_context`.
    69	 *
    70	 * Everything here is pure. No clock: `answerBinding` takes its timestamp. No id
    71	 * generation: it takes its ids. That is what makes an acceptance decision
    72	 * reproducible from the log months later, which is the property the whole
    73	 * correction story rests on.
    74	 */
    75	
    76	/* ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    77	 * The decision
    78	 * ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── */
    79	
    80	/** Who a commitment's sentence came from. */
    81	export type CommitmentAttribution =
    82	  /** The owner wrote the message bearing it — "I'll finish it tomorrow". */
    83	  | 'self'
    84	  /** Somebody else did — "Justin will handle it". Nobody gets committed by it. */
    85	  | 'third_party';
    86	
    87	export type AcceptanceVerdict =
    88	  /** Accept it now, as `~`. */
    89	  | 'auto_accept'
    90	  /** Keep it staged. `visibility` says where it shows. */
    91	  | 'pending'
    92	  /** Drop it. Never shown, never stored as a live proposal. */
    93	  | 'discard';
    94	
    95	export type AcceptanceVisibility =
    96	  /** It became an object; nothing is pending. */
    97	  | 'accepted'
    98	  /** Current state, marked unconfirmed. Never in Needs-you. */
    99	  | 'quiet'
   100	  /** Needs-you: somebody has to act on it. */
   101	  | 'needs_you'
   102	  /** Nowhere. */
   103	  | 'none';
   104	
   105	/**
   106	 * Which cell of the matrix fired. One name per cell, so tests can pin them.
   107	 *
   108	 * **Data, so a coverage test can iterate it instead of restating it.** r4's
   109	 * blind review found the shape this closes: `acceptance.test.ts` carried a test
   110	 * titled *"reaches every rule name the type declares"* whose supposedly complete
   111	 * list omitted `receipt_not_certifiable`, so the test passed and would have gone
   112	 * on passing if that engine behaviour were deleted. A hand-written list catches
   113	 * a rule that disappears from the source and is blind to one that appears; the
   114	 * derived list is blind the other way. Deriving the *type* from the data makes
   115	 * them the same object, so a test iterating it is exhaustive by construction.
   116	 *
   117	 *  - `missing_message_context` — no window, an empty one, or one with no words
   118	 *    in any body. A model reading judged without the messages it cites.
   119	 *  - `provenance_failed` — a `reject`-severity receipt problem.
   120	 *  - `receipt_not_certifiable` — a `refer`-severity one: the check declines to
   121	 *    rule either way, so it is never auto-accepted and never discarded.
   122	 *  - `duplicate_of_accepted` — the room already accepted this, from these
   123	 *    messages.
   124	 *  - `below_theta_min` — too weak to be worth anybody's attention, with nothing
   125	 *    wrong in the receipt.
   126	 *  - `theta_band` — between θ_min and θ_auto: shown quietly, never Needs-you.
   127	 *  - `auto_accept` — at or above θ_auto, for a type that may auto-accept.
   128	 *  - `never_auto_accepts` — at or above θ_auto, for a type that never does at
   129	 *    any confidence. Named for the *rule* rather than the type: it was
   130	 *    `decision_never_auto`, which misnamed every non-decision that reached it,
   131	 *    and since r5 three types are in that row.
   132	 *  - `third_party_commitment` — an obligation somebody else's sentence put a
   133	 *    name on. It goes to the named owner.
   134	 *  - `human_proposer` — a person staged it, so θ does not apply.
   135	 */
   136	export const ACCEPTANCE_RULE_NAMES = [
   137	  'missing_message_context',
   138	  'provenance_failed',
   139	  'receipt_not_certifiable',
   140	  'duplicate_of_accepted',
   141	  'below_theta_min',
   142	  'theta_band',
   143	  'auto_accept',
   144	  'never_auto_accepts',
   145	  'third_party_commitment',
   146	  'human_proposer',
   147	] as const;
   148	
   149	export type AcceptanceRuleName = (typeof ACCEPTANCE_RULE_NAMES)[number];
   150	
   151	export interface AcceptanceDecision {
   152	  verdict: AcceptanceVerdict;
   153	  visibility: AcceptanceVisibility;
   154	  /** The named owner who must confirm, for a third-party commitment. */
   155	  awaitingConfirmFrom: Id | null;
   156	  /** The matrix cell. */
   157	  rule: AcceptanceRuleName;
   158	  /** Why, in words a room could be shown. */
   159	  reason: string;
   160	  /** The confident line for this type. */
   161	  thetaAuto: number;
   162	  /** Whether crossing it may accept, or only surface. */
   163	  autoAcceptAvailable: boolean;
   164	  thetaMin: number;
   165	  /** Only set for commitments. */
   166	  attribution: CommitmentAttribution | null;
   167	  /** The accepted object this duplicates, when that is why it was discarded. */
   168	  duplicateOf: Id | null;
   169	}
   170	
   171	/** An already-accepted object, as the deduplicator needs it. */
   172	export interface AcceptedObjectRef {
   173	  objectId: Id;
   174	  type: AcceptedObjectType;
   175	  /** `statement` / `question` / `title`, whichever the type carries. */
   176	  text: string;
   177	  /** The messages it was drawn from. */
   178	  messageIds: readonly Id[];
   179	}
   180	
   181	export interface AcceptanceContext {
   182	  config?: AcceptanceConfig;
   183	  /**
   184	   * The window's messages — **required, and non-empty**, and the reason is the
   185	   * whole of round 1's second blocking finding. Commitment attribution and
   186	   * provenance validation cannot be done without them, and in round 1 that meant
   187	   * they silently were not done: no messages, no problems, auto-accept. A model
   188	   * proposal judged with no window is discarded now.
   189	   *
   190	   * The type cannot say "non-empty", so the check does: `[]` is refused with the
   191	   * same rule as `undefined`. Round 2's gauntlet found the version that only
   192	   * asked about `undefined`, and an empty array is not a smaller window — it is
   193	   * the same absence with a different spelling.
   194	   *
   195	   * A human-staged proposal does not go through θ at all, so it survives an
   196	   * empty window; there is nothing to check when the person staging the reading
   197	   * is the receipt.
   198	   */
   199	  messages: readonly ProvenanceMessage[];
   200	  /**
   201	   * What the room has already accepted, for deduplication.
   202	   *
   203	   * This is where dedup belongs. The spike tested the alternative directly —
   204	   * putting a compressed accepted-state block in the extraction prompt — and it
   205	   * dedups well (zero re-proposed objects) at a cost of collapsing recall from
   206	   * 19 objects to 11, losing the dispute edge and the `disputed` flag, and
   207	   * producing no cross-window relations at all. It is a de-duplication input,
   208	   * not a comprehension aid. Doing it here costs nothing and is testable
   209	   * without a model.
   210	   */
   211	  acceptedObjects?: readonly AcceptedObjectRef[];
   212	}
   213	
   214	/** The person a payload puts on the hook, if any. */
   215	export function payloadAttributedTo(
   216	  type: AcceptedObjectType,
   217	  payload: Record<string, unknown>,
   218	): Id | null {
   219	  const key = type === 'claim' ? 'claimant' : type === 'commitment' ? 'owner' : null;
   220	  if (key === null) return null;
   221	  const value = payload[key];
   222	  return typeof value === 'string' ? value : null;
   223	}
   224	
   225	/**
   226	 * Did the owner of this commitment write the message it was read out of?
   227	 *
   228	 * **The message bearing the sentence, not any cited message.** Round 1's
   229	 * gauntlet: an owner who authored *any* cited message counted as a
   230	 * self-statement, so padding `provenance` with one unrelated message the owner
   231	 * happened to write turned "Justin will handle it" into "Justin said he would
   232	 * handle it" and auto-accepted an obligation nobody agreed to. The quote names
   233	 * the bearing message; its author is the only authorship that answers the
   234	 * question.
   235	 *
   236	 * With no quote, or no window, the answer is `third_party`, deliberately. #4's
   237	 * rule is "nobody gets committed by someone else's sentence", and the only way
   238	 * to honour it without evidence is to ask the named person. An unproven
   239	 * self-statement is a third-party statement.
   240	 */
   241	export function commitmentAttribution(
   242	  owner: Id,
   243	  citedMessageIds: readonly Id[],
   244	  messages: readonly ProvenanceMessage[] | undefined,
   245	  quote: string | null | undefined,
   246	): CommitmentAttribution {
   247	  // Absent, empty, and empty-of-content are one state: nobody supplied the words.
   248	  if (!messages?.some((message) => hasContent(message.body))) return 'third_party';
   249	  if (!hasContent(quote)) return 'third_party';
   250	  const cited = new Set(citedMessageIds);
   251	  const citedMessages = messages.filter((message) => cited.has(message.id));
   252	  const bearing = bearingMessage(quote, citedMessages);
   253	  return bearing !== null && bearing.authorId === owner ? 'self' : 'third_party';
   254	}
   255	
   256	/**
   257	 * Is this reading something the room already accepted?
   258	 *
   259	 * Same sentence **and** provenance overlap, both required. Either alone is a bad
   260	 * matcher: two different decisions drawn from one message share provenance
   261	 * completely, and two readings of one subsystem share most of their words.
   262	 *
   263	 * ## Why this is not a similarity score any more
   264	 *
   265	 * **r5, from the audit of what every documented limit does with an input inside
   266	 * it — and this one was not on anybody's list.** `escalation.ts` says of its
   267	 * stopword table, in bold: *"This list decides which model reads a window. It
   268	 * has not decided whether a reading becomes a fact since r4, and it must never
   269	 * do so again."* It still did, through here. This function measured similarity
   270	 * with `contentTokens`, which drops stopwords, deduplicates and imposes a
   271	 * three-character floor — and `not`, `all` and `some` are all on that list. A
   272	 * duplicate is **discarded**, so:
   273	 *
   274	 * | already accepted, from msg_1     | newly read, from msg_1              | r4 verdict |
   275	 * | -------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | ---------- |
   276	 * | The migration is reversible      | The migration is **not** reversible | discarded  |
   277	 * | **All** services restart cleanly | **Some** services restart cleanly   | discarded  |
   278	 *
   279	 * That is r3's gauntlet finding — a similarity measure accepting the negation of
   280	 * the thing it validates — surviving in the one path nobody re-read, and it is
   281	 * *worse* here than there: the reading is not referred or refused, it is
   282	 * destroyed, and what it was contradicting stays on the record. A correction the
   283	 * room can never see is the failure this whole ticket exists to prevent.
   284	 *
   285	 * So the test is the alignment the receipt already uses, over routing tokens: a
   286	 * duplicate is a **re-proposal of the same sentence**, with nothing added and
   287	 * nothing dropped on either side. `duplicateThreshold` is gone rather than
   288	 * tuned — `RETRO.md`: when a value invites a bypass, deleting it invites
   289	 * neither. A near-duplicate now costs a person one click, which is the cheap
   290	 * side of this trade; the expensive side deleted a contradiction in silence.
   291	 *
   292	 * Routing tokens rather than receipt tokens, deliberately: case, fullwidth
   293	 * spellings and markdown are noise to "is this the same sentence again", and
   294	 * being lossy *here* means firing more often, which for the receipt path is the
   295	 * dangerous direction and for a re-proposal is the harmless one — a text that
   296	 * differs only in case is the same reading twice.
   297	 */
   298	export function findDuplicate(
   299	  type: AcceptedObjectType,
   300	  text: string,
   301	  messageIds: readonly Id[],
   302	  accepted: readonly AcceptedObjectRef[],
   303	): AcceptedObjectRef | null {
   304	  const wanted = routingTokens(text);
   305	  if (wanted.length === 0) return null;
   306	  const cited = new Set(messageIds);
   307	  for (const candidate of accepted) {
   308	    if (candidate.type !== type) continue;
   309	    if (!candidate.messageIds.some((id) => cited.has(id))) continue;
   310	    const have = routingTokens(candidate.text);
   311	    if (have.length === 0) continue;
   312	    // Symmetric by construction: `borne` requires both sides accounted for, so
   313	    // neither "the new one restates the old" nor the reverse is enough alone.
   314	    if (alignTokens(have, wanted).borne) return candidate;
   315	  }
   316	  return null;
   317	}
   318	
   319	/**
   320	 * #4's matrix, applied to one staged reading.
   321	 *
   322	 * The table this implements, exactly — one row per cell, and
   323	 * `acceptance.test.ts` has one test per row:
   324	 *
   325	 * | type                     | c < θ_min | θ_min ≤ c < θ_auto | c ≥ θ_auto            |
   326	 * | ------------------------ | --------- | ------------------ | --------------------- |
   327	 * | claim                    | discard   | pending, quiet     | auto-accept           |
   328	 * | open_question            | discard   | pending, quiet     | auto-accept           |
   329	 * | objective                | discard   | pending, quiet     | auto-accept           |
   330	 * | commitment, self-stated  | discard   | pending, quiet     | auto-accept           |
   331	 * | commitment, third-party  | discard   | pending, quiet     | pending, owner confirm|
   332	 * | decision                 | discard   | pending, quiet     | pending, Needs-you    |
   333	 *
   334	 * …with one cell in front of all of them: **no window, no verdict**. A model
   335	 * proposal judged without the messages it cites is discarded, because the
   336	 * alternative — an empty problem set read as a clean receipt — is how a wrong
   337	 * citation becomes an accepted fact.
   338	 *
   339	 * …and one cell across all of them, above θ_min: **a receipt nothing can rule
   340	 * on is pending, quiet**. Not accepted, because a machine may not act on a
   341	 * quote it cannot certify; not discarded, because the discrepancy is exactly
   342	 * what a person needs to see. `receipt_not_certifiable`.
   343	 *
   344	 * Two cells are worth defending.
   345	 *
   346	 * **Decision at c ≥ θ_auto is Needs-you, not quiet.** #4 says a pending
   347	 * proposal shows quietly "never in Needs-you unless it blocks something", and #6
   348	 * says `needs_decision` is "a decision proposal awaiting you". Both are right,
   349	 * and the θ band is where they meet: a decision the pass is unsure about shows
   350	 * quietly, and one it is confident about is exactly the thing a person should be
   351	 * asked to confirm. A confident decision proposal *does* block something — the
   352	 * room's current state is wrong until somebody rules on it.
   353	 *
   354	 * **Third-party commitment never auto-accepts, at any confidence.** Not a
   355	 * threshold: a rule. The spike could not test it (one commitment in six runs,
   356	 * self-attributed, on a public RFC thread where nobody says "Ryan will handle
   357	 * it"), so it is implemented from #4's text and is the part of this file most
   358	 * in need of the working-team corpus #24 asks for.
   359	 */
   360	export function decideAcceptance(
   361	  proposal: Proposal | StoredProposal,
   362	  context: AcceptanceContext,
   363	): AcceptanceDecision {
   364	  const config = context.config ?? defaultAcceptanceConfig;
   365	  const rule = config[proposal.type];
   366	  const payload = proposal.payload as unknown as Record<string, unknown>;
   367	  const text = payloadText(proposal.type, payload);
   368	  const attributedTo = payloadAttributedTo(proposal.type, payload);
   369	  const messages = context.messages as readonly ProvenanceMessage[] | undefined;
   370	
   371	  const base = {
   372	    thetaAuto: rule.thetaAuto,
   373	    thetaMin: rule.thetaMin,
   374	    autoAcceptAvailable: rule.autoAccept,
   375	    attribution: null,
   376	    duplicateOf: null,
   377	    awaitingConfirmFrom: null,
   378	  } as const;
   379	
   380	  // ── No window, no verdict ────────────────────────────────────────────────
   381	  //
   382	  // Fail-closed, and loudly: this is a caller bug, not a reading defect. The
   383	  // failure it replaces was silent in the other direction.
   384	  //
   385	  // **Empty counts as absent.** Round 2's gauntlet found the `undefined`-only
   386	  // form of this check: `messages: []` walked past the door marked "required",
   387	  // and everything downstream then found nothing wrong because there was nothing
   388	  // to look in. The two spellings describe the same state of the world — nobody
   389	  // supplied the messages this reading cites — so they get the same answer.
   390	  //
   391	  // **And empty of content counts as empty.** Round 3's gauntlet found the
   392	  // `length`-only form: one message whose `body` is `""` makes the array
   393	  // non-empty and supplies nothing, which is the same trick one level in. The
   394	  // question is whether anybody supplied words, so the test is `hasContent`.
   395	  const windowIsAbsent =
   396	    messages === undefined || messages.length === 0 || !messages.some((m) => hasContent(m.body));
   397	  if (proposal.proposer.kind === 'model' && windowIsAbsent) {
   398	    return {
   399	      ...base,
   400	      verdict: 'discard',
   401	      visibility: 'none',
   402	      rule: 'missing_message_context',
   403	      reason:
   404	        messages === undefined
   405	          ? 'no message window was supplied, so the receipt could not be checked — a model reading is never accepted on trust; supply the messages it cites'
   406	          : messages.length === 0
   407	            ? 'an empty message window was supplied, so the receipt could not be checked — an empty window is not a window; a model reading is never accepted on trust'
   408	            : `a message window of ${messages.length} message${messages.length === 1 ? '' : 's'} was supplied and not one of them has any words in it, so the receipt could not be checked — a window with nothing in it is not a window; a model reading is never accepted on trust`,
   409	    };
   410	  }
   411	
   412	  // ── The receipt, before anything else ────────────────────────────────────
   413	  //
   414	  // A reading whose citation is wrong is worse than no reading: the citation is
   415	  // what a person clicks to check it, and a wrong one survives casual review.
   416	  // The spike's worst output — a claim at 0.98 confidence attributed to a man
   417	  // who never said it — fails here and nowhere else.
   418	  //
   419	  // Only `reject`-severity problems discard. A `reclassify` one — an owner who
   420	  // did not write the message bearing the sentence — is not a defect in the
   421	  // reading, it *is* the third-party commitment case, and it routes below
   422	  // rather than dying here.
   423	  const problems: readonly ProvenanceProblem[] =
   424	    messages && messages.length > 0
   425	      ? validateProposalProvenance(
   426	          {
   427	            type: proposal.type,
   428	            provenance: proposal.provenance,
   429	            quote: proposal.quote,
   430	            proposer: proposal.proposer,
   431	            attributedTo,
   432	            // The sentence being staged. Without it the quote can be verbatim,
   433	            // correctly attributed, and about something else entirely — r2's
   434	            // gauntlet, major 1.
   435	            statement: text,
   436	          },
   437	          messages,
   438	        )
   439	      : [];
   440	  const rejecting = rejectingProblems(problems);
   441	  if (rejecting.length > 0) {
   442	    return {
   443	      ...base,
   444	      verdict: 'discard',
   445	      visibility: 'none',
   446	      rule: 'provenance_failed',
   447	      reason: `demoted below θ_min: ${rejecting.map((problem) => problem.detail).join('; ')}`,
   448	    };
   449	  }
   450	
   451	  // ── Already in the room ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
   452	  const duplicate = context.acceptedObjects
   453	    ? findDuplicate(proposal.type, text, proposal.provenance, context.acceptedObjects)
   454	    : null;
   455	  if (duplicate) {
   456	    return {
   457	      ...base,
   458	      verdict: 'discard',
   459	      visibility: 'none',
   460	      rule: 'duplicate_of_accepted',
   461	      duplicateOf: duplicate.objectId,
   462	      reason: `the room already accepted "${duplicate.objectId}" from the same messages, saying the same thing`,
   463	    };
   464	  }
   465	
   466	  // ── A person staged this ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
   467	  //
   468	  // θ does not apply. #4's thresholds calibrate *extraction* confidence, and a
   469	  // person's self-report is not that number — it is not a number at all. A
   470	  // human-staged reading goes to the room to be judged, which is what staging
   471	  // means. (A person writing a fact outright uses `answerBinding` or direct
   472	  // acceptance; they do not need a proposal.)
   473	  if (proposal.proposer.kind === 'human') {
   474	    // …with one exception that is not about θ at all: a person naming *somebody
   475	    // else* on a commitment is still somebody else's sentence, and #4's rule
   476	    // does not care whether a machine or a colleague wrote it. The named owner
   477	    // is the one asked, and that is what the attention panel reads to decide
   478	    // whose confirm this is.
   479	    const staged = proposal.proposer.userId;
   480	    const namesAnother =
   481	      proposal.type === 'commitment' && attributedTo !== null && attributedTo !== staged;
   482	    return {
   483	      ...base,
   484	      verdict: 'pending',
   485	      visibility: 'needs_you',
   486	      attribution: proposal.type === 'commitment' ? (namesAnother ? 'third_party' : 'self') : null,
   487	      awaitingConfirmFrom: namesAnother ? attributedTo : null,
   488	      rule: 'human_proposer',
   489	      reason: namesAnother
   490	        ? `staged by user "${staged}", who named "${attributedTo}" as owner — it waits for "${attributedTo}" to confirm; nobody gets committed by someone else's sentence (#4), whoever wrote it`
   491	        : `staged by user "${staged}" — a person's reading goes to the room to be accepted, not through θ`,
   492	    };
   493	  }
   494	
   495	  const attribution =
   496	    proposal.type === 'commitment'
   497	      ? commitmentAttribution(attributedTo ?? '', proposal.provenance, messages, proposal.quote)
   498	      : null;
   499	
   500	  // ── The receipt this check declines to rule on ───────────────────────────
   501	  //
   502	  // **Above the θ_min discard since r5, and the two cases are different
   503	  // questions rather than two heights on one scale.**
   504	  //
   505	  // r4 put this below the discard and defended it: a weak reading should be
   506	  // dropped rather than promoted into the panel by a receipt problem. That
   507	  // argument is right about *one* input and r4 applied it to two. θ_min answers
   508	  // "is this reading worth somebody's attention" — a property of the model's own
   509	  // self-report, and a weak self-report with a clean receipt is noise, so it is
   510	  // still discarded below. A `refer` answers something else entirely: **the
   511	  // receipt and the statement disagree, and the disagreement was detected.**
   512	  // That is evidence about the record, not weakness in the reading.
   513	  //
   514	  // The input that made the difference visible: quote "Bob will not deploy
   515	  // production Friday", statement "Bob will deploy production Friday",
   516	  // confidence 0.4999. r4 discarded it as too weak to show — destroying the
   517	  // exact discrepancy the receipt exists to make visible, one thousandth of a
   518	  // point below a line that has nothing to do with what was found. The
   519	  // inversion is not less real for having been proposed timidly.
   520	  //
   521	  // Still **above everything that can accept**, so there is no path from here to
   522	  // `auto_accept`.
   523	  const referring = referringProblems(problems);
   524	  if (referring.length > 0) {
   525	    return {
   526	      ...base,
   527	      attribution,
   528	      verdict: 'pending',
   529	      visibility: 'quiet',
   530	      rule: 'receipt_not_certifiable',
   531	      reason: `not accepted on a machine's word, and not thrown away: ${referring.map((problem) => problem.detail).join('; ')}`,
   532	    };
   533	  }
   534	
   535	  // ── Below the discard line ───────────────────────────────────────────────
   536	  //
   537	  // Reached only with a receipt nothing found fault with, which is what makes
   538	  // discarding safe: there is no detected discrepancy here to destroy, only a
   539	  // model that is not convinced by its own reading.
   540	  if (proposal.confidence < rule.thetaMin) {
   541	    return {
   542	      ...base,
   543	      attribution,
   544	      verdict: 'discard',
   545	      visibility: 'none',
   546	      rule: 'below_theta_min',
   547	      reason: `confidence ${proposal.confidence} is below θ_min ${rule.thetaMin} for ${proposal.type} — discarded rather than shown; an unconvincing "~" still costs someone's attention, and its receipt held, so nothing is being thrown away except a weak reading`,
   548	    };
   549	  }
   550	
   551	  // ── The band: θ_min ≤ c < θ_auto ─────────────────────────────────────────
   552	  //
   553	  // One cell for every type, decisions included. Shown quietly in current state
   554	  // as unconfirmed, never in Needs-you — #4, and the reasoning is that an
   555	  // uncertain reading is not worth a person's turn, only their glance.
   556	  if (proposal.confidence < rule.thetaAuto) {
   557	    return {
   558	      ...base,
   559	      attribution,
   560	      verdict: 'pending',
   561	      visibility: 'quiet',
   562	      rule: 'theta_band',
   563	      reason: `confidence ${proposal.confidence} is between θ_min ${rule.thetaMin} and θ_auto ${rule.thetaAuto} for ${proposal.type} — shown quietly in current state as unconfirmed, never in Needs-you unless it blocks something`,
   564	    };
   565	  }
   566	
   567	  // ── At or above θ_auto, and somebody else's name is on it ────────────────
   568	  //
   569	  // **Before the never-auto-accepts row since r5**, because r5 moved commitment
   570	  // into that row and the general refusal would otherwise have swallowed the
   571	  // specific one. Both end in `pending, needs_you`; only this one knows *whose*
   572	  // Needs-you, and `awaitingConfirmFrom` is what the attention panel reads to
   573	  // put it in front of the person being committed rather than the room.
   574	  if (proposal.type === 'commitment' && attribution === 'third_party') {
   575	    return {
   576	      ...base,
   577	      attribution,
   578	      verdict: 'pending',
   579	      visibility: 'needs_you',
   580	      awaitingConfirmFrom: attributedTo,
   581	      rule: 'third_party_commitment',
   582	      reason: `"${attributedTo}" is named as owner but did not write the message this was read out of — surfaced to them to confirm, and accepted only on that confirm; nobody gets committed by someone else's sentence (#4)`,
   583	    };
   584	  }
   585	
   586	  // ── At or above θ_auto, but this type never auto-accepts ─────────────────
   587	  //
   588	  // Three types are in this row since r5: decision (#4's absolute), commitment
   589	  // and objective (see `DEFAULT_ACCEPTANCE_RULES`). The rule is read off the
   590	  // table rather than named here, so a type joining or leaving the row changes
   591	  // one line of data.
   592	  if (!rule.autoAccept) {
   593	    return {
   594	      ...base,
   595	      attribution,
   596	      verdict: 'pending',
   597	      visibility: 'needs_you',
   598	      rule: 'never_auto_accepts',
   599	      reason: `a ${proposal.type} never auto-accepts at any confidence (#4) — at ${proposal.confidence} the pass is confident, so it goes to Needs-you for a person to accept or decline; the room's current state is unsettled until somebody rules on it`,
   600	    };
   601	  }
   602	
   603	  return {
   604	    ...base,
   605	    attribution,
   606	    verdict: 'auto_accept',
   607	    visibility: 'accepted',
   608	    rule: 'auto_accept',
   609	    reason: `confidence ${proposal.confidence} is at or above θ_auto ${rule.thetaAuto} for ${proposal.type} — accepted as "~"; nothing model-accepted renders as a fact`,
   610	  };
   611	}
   612	
   613	/* ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   614	 * Answer-binding
   615	 * ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── */
   616	
   617	/**
   618	 * "Your next message resolves it, nothing is inferred."
   619	 *
   620	 * The one path that reaches an accepted Decision without a model in it. A person
   621	 * clicks an open question, writes a reply, and that reply *is* the answer — no
   622	 * proposal is staged, no confidence is computed, nothing is extracted. #4 names
   623	 * this as one of exactly two ways a decision is ever accepted.
   624	 *
   625	 * It is a **command**, not an interpretation: this turns the command into the
   626	 * two events the reducer will fold, and both the object id and the event ids
   627	 * come from the caller. No clock, no uuid, no randomness — hand it the same
   628	 * command twice and you get byte-identical events, which is what makes the whole
   629	 * path replayable.
   630	 *
   631	 * **The actor is not in here.** It is the same trusted argument the reducer
   632	 * takes, for the same reason: a command that carried its own actor would be a
   633	 * self-declared human, and this is the one path that mints an accepted decision.
   634	 */
   635	export interface AnswerBindingCommand {
   636	  /** Event timestamp; also the objects' created/updated time. */
   637	  at: Timestamp;
   638	  roomId: Id;
   639	  /** The open question being answered. */
   640	  questionObjectId: Id;
   641	  /** What the answer is. A decision or a claim — those are what `answers` targets. */
   642	  answer:
   643	    | { type: 'decision'; objectId: Id; payload: DecisionPayload }
   644	    | { type: 'claim'; objectId: Id; payload: ClaimPayload };
   645	  /** The message that carried the answer, and any others it rests on. */
   646	  messageIds: readonly Id[];
   647	  /** Caller-supplied ids: purity is the point. */
   648	  ids: { acceptEventId: Id; relationEventId: Id; relationId: Id };
   649	  /** Carried onto the created object. */
   650	  objectiveId?: Id | null;
   651	}
   652	
   653	/**
   654	 * Why this binding cannot be performed against this state, or `null`.
   655	 *
   656	 * Checked here so a caller can refuse before minting events, and checked again
   657	 * by the reducer because a check that only runs upstream is not a check.
   658	 *
   659	 * The ordering rule is the one worth explaining. The two events share a
   660	 * timestamp, so the canonical `(at, id)` order breaks the tie on the *ids the
     1	import type { Actor } from './common.js';
     2	import {
     3	  type ProvenanceMessage,
     4	  referringProblems,
     5	  rejectingProblems,
     6	  validateProposalProvenance,
     7	} from './escalation.js';
     8	import { hasContent, isBlank } from './matching.js';
     9	import { type AcceptedObject, type AcceptedObjectType, objectStatement } from './objects.js';
    10	import { decideSupersession, MODEL_ACCEPTANCE_FLOOR } from './policy.js';
    11	import type { Proposer, StoredProposal } from './proposal.js';
    12	import { canonicalJson } from './state.js';
    13	
    14	/**
    15	 * Who is allowed to do what, and on what evidence — the *floor* under the
    16	 * acceptance rules, enforced where nothing can route around it.
    17	 *
    18	 * Issue #4 resolved acceptance per type. Some of that matrix is policy that
    19	 * belongs to the θ engine; the rest is the trust boundary itself, and a boundary
    20	 * enforced only in the layer above the reducer is a boundary that a second
    21	 * writer, a replay, or a bug walks around. Those rows live here, and the reducer
    22	 * refuses to fold an event that breaks them.
    23	 *
    24	 * #4's matrix, and where each row is enforced:
    25	 *
    26	 * | rule (#4)                                               | who may | where     |
    27	 * | ------------------------------------------------------- | ------- | --------- |
    28	 * | Claims auto-accept at confidence ≥ θ                     | model   | **here**  |
    29	 * | OpenQuestions auto-accept at confidence ≥ θ              | model   | **here**  |
    30	 * | **Commitments never auto-accept** (r5)                   | human   | **here**  |
    31	 * | **Objectives never auto-accept** (r5)                    | human   | **here**  |
    32	 * | **Decisions never auto-accept**                          | human   | **here**  |
    33	 * | A claim becomes `verified`                               | human   | **here**  |
    34	 * | Supersession, split by the type being retired            | per policy | **here** |
    35	 * | **Corrections (amend / retract / restore / …)**          | human   | **here**  |
    36	 * | **Acceptance citing no proposal at all**                 | human   | **here**  |
    37	 * | **Declaring a question answered**                        | human   | **here**  |
    38	 *
    39	 * ## What round 2 moved down here, and why
    40	 *
    41	 * Every row above that used to say "#21 (θ)" or "the engine" now says **here**.
    42	 * Round 1's gauntlet found the same shape twice:
    43	 *
    44	 *  1. **The actor was forgeable.** Every gate below reads an actor, and the
    45	 *     actor used to be part of the event payload — so a worker could declare
    46	 *     itself human and walk through all of them. The actor now arrives out of
    47	 *     band (`TrustedContext`), derived from the authenticated session, and the
    48	 *     event schema has nowhere to put one.
    49	 *  2. **The receipt checks were opt-in.** θ, quote-matching, and "did the person
    50	 *     named actually say this" lived in `acceptance.ts`, which the layer that
    51	 *     mints events calls *if it remembers to* and which returned "no problems"
    52	 *     when handed no messages. A check that fails open when its input is missing
    53	 *     is a manner, not a boundary. They are conditions of folding now:
    54	 *     `acceptanceReceiptRefusal` runs on every non-human acceptance, and an
    55	 *     acceptance whose messages the reducer cannot see is refused rather than
    56	 *     waved through.
    57	 *
    58	 * Two things are still deliberately left open, so the omission is a decision:
    59	 *  - `proposal_recorded` is open to every actor. Recording a reading is not
    60	 *    asserting it; the whole trust model is that staging is free and acceptance
    61	 *    is not.
    62	 *  - `proposal_rejected` is not a correction verb — withdrawing a staged reading
    63	 *    destroys nothing — but it is narrowed by `ProposalBindingGate`: a model may
    64	 *    retire *its own* reading, not somebody else's.
    65	 *
    66	 * `acceptance.ts` still exists and is still stricter: it runs over richer inputs
    67	 * (what the room already accepted, the whole window) and decides what a worker
    68	 * should emit at all. It reads the same θ table this file does, so the two
    69	 * cannot invert.
    70	 *
    71	 * ## What this floor enforces *independently*, and what it shares
    72	 *
    73	 * r4's receipt claimed two boundaries without qualification, and r4's blind
    74	 * review was right to refuse it: `acceptanceReceiptRefusal` below calls the same
    75	 * `validateProposalProvenance` the engine does, so a defect in quote semantics
    76	 * is a defect in both. The claim is scoped rather than repeated:
    77	 *
    78	 *  - **Independent of the engine, and unreachable from it**: the actor gates
    79	 *    above; that a cited proposal exists, is open, and matches the object's type
    80	 *    and room; that a non-human acted on its own reading; `MODEL_ACCEPTANCE_FLOOR`;
    81	 *    payload equality; provenance equality; that a window exists; that a quote
    82	 *    exists. Delete `acceptance.ts` entirely and every one of those still holds.
    83	 *  - **Shared, by design**: what the quote *means* — one implementation, two
    84	 *    call sites, because two implementations of "does this quote bear this
    85	 *    sentence" would be two answers to one question and `policy.ts` exists to
    86	 *    say why that is the defect rather than the redundancy.
    87	 */
    88	export type HumanOnlyGate =
    89	  /** `object_accepted` with `provenance.proposalId === null`. */
    90	  | 'direct_acceptance'
    91	  /** `object_accepted` for a `decision`, proposal or no proposal. */
    92	  | 'decision_acceptance'
    93	  /**
    94	   * `object_accepted` for a `commitment`. **r5.** #44's fact-check drove a model
    95	   * actor through the whole stack with `{statement: 'Bob will deploy production
    96	   * Friday', owner: 'user_bob'}` and it landed with zero issues: the quote was
    97	   * real, the author was real, and the attribution rules read it as self-stated
    98	   * because the bearing message's author id equalled the owner. Nothing asked
    99	   * whether a machine may put an obligation on a named person at all.
   100	   */
   101	  | 'commitment_acceptance'
   102	  /**
   103	   * `object_accepted` for an `objective`. **r5.** An objective is what
   104	   * everything else is filed under, and `decideSupersession` already reserves
   105	   * *retiring* one to a person — a gate on the way out and none on the way in is
   106	   * a front door with the back door open.
   107	   */
   108	  | 'objective_acceptance'
   109	  /** Any transition of a claim to `verification: 'verified'`. */
   110	  | 'claim_verification'
   111	  /** `supersedes` pointed at a type the supersession policy reserves to people. */
   112	  | 'supersession'
   113	  /** An `answers` edge — declaring a question settled. */
   114	  | 'answer_relation'
   115	  /** `object_corrected`, every verb. */
   116	  | 'correction';
   117	
   118	/**
   119	 * The gate a non-human actor hits when it tries to mint this type, or `null`
   120	 * when the type is one a machine may mint.
   121	 *
   122	 * **Derived from the same θ table `MODEL_ACCEPTANCE_FLOOR` is derived from**, so
   123	 * the named refusal and the unreachable number cannot drift: a type that stops
   124	 * auto-accepting acquires a gate here in the same commit, and `policy.test.ts`
   125	 * asserts the two agree for every type rather than trusting this switch.
   126	 *
   127	 * A gate *and* a floor for the same rule is deliberate. The floor alone would
   128	 * refuse these acceptances — `+Infinity` is unreachable — but it would refuse
   129	 * them as "confidence 0.95 is below the floor of Infinity", which tells a room
   130	 * nothing about why. `RETRO.md`: a rule applied at one site is not a rule, and a
   131	 * refusal that does not name itself is a dead end.
   132	 */
   133	export function modelMintingGate(type: AcceptedObjectType): HumanOnlyGate | null {
   134	  switch (type) {
   135	    case 'decision':
   136	      return 'decision_acceptance';
   137	    case 'commitment':
   138	      return 'commitment_acceptance';
   139	    case 'objective':
   140	      return 'objective_acceptance';
   141	    case 'claim':
   142	    case 'open_question':
   143	      return null;
   144	    default: {
   145	      const exhaustive: never = type;
   146	      // A type nobody has classified is not a type a machine may mint.
   147	      return JSON.stringify(exhaustive) === '' ? null : 'direct_acceptance';
   148	    }
   149	  }
   150	}
   151	
   152	/** The one predicate every gate is built from. */
   153	export function isHuman(actor: Actor): boolean {
   154	  return actor.kind === 'human';
   155	}
   156	
   157	/**
   158	 * The refusal text for a gate. Kept beside the matrix so the reason a room
   159	 * sees and the rule that produced it cannot drift apart, and so every message
   160	 * names both what was refused and the route that stays open — a refusal that
   161	 * does not say what to do instead is a dead end.
   162	 */
   163	export function humanOnlyRefusal(
   164	  gate: HumanOnlyGate,
   165	  actor: Actor,
   166	  subject: string,
   167	  retiredType?: AcceptedObjectType,
   168	): string {
   169	  const kind = actor.kind;
   170	  switch (gate) {
   171	    case 'direct_acceptance':
   172	      return `${subject} was accepted with no proposal by a ${kind} actor — only a human may accept an object directly; a ${kind} actor must record a proposal and have it accepted`;
   173	    case 'decision_acceptance':
   174	      return `${subject} is a decision accepted by a ${kind} actor — a decision never auto-accepts (issue #4): a ${kind} actor may propose one, but only a human may accept it`;
   175	    case 'commitment_acceptance':
   176	      return `${subject} is a commitment accepted by a ${kind} actor — accepting it writes an obligation onto a named person who never agreed to it, and #4's rule is that nobody gets committed by someone else's sentence; a ${kind} actor may propose the commitment and let the person named, or a person in the room, accept it`;
   177	    case 'objective_acceptance':
   178	      return `${subject} is an objective accepted by a ${kind} actor — an objective is what everything else in the room is filed under, and retiring one already needs a person (#4's supersession split), so minting one does too; a ${kind} actor may propose it and let a person accept`;
   179	    case 'claim_verification':
   180	      return `${subject} would become a verified claim on a ${kind} actor's word — only a human may move a claim to "verified"; a ${kind} actor may accept it as unverified or disputed`;
   181	    case 'supersession':
   182	      return `${subject} retires an accepted ${retiredType ?? 'object'} on a ${kind} actor's word — ${retiredType ? decideSupersession(retiredType).reason : 'this type needs a human'}; a ${kind} actor may propose the replacement and let a person retire it`;
   183	    case 'answer_relation':
   184	      return `${subject} declares an open question answered on a ${kind} actor's word — only a human may bind an answer (#4: a decision reaches the room through answer-binding or an explicit accept, never through inference); a ${kind} actor may propose the answer and let a person bind it`;
   185	    case 'correction':
   186	      return `${subject} was corrected by a ${kind} actor — corrections (amend, retract, restore) are human-only in v1: a correction rewrites what the room already accepted`;
   187	    default: {
   188	      const exhaustive: never = gate;
   189	      return `unknown authority gate ${JSON.stringify(exhaustive)}`;
   190	    }
   191	  }
   192	}
   193	
   194	/**
   195	 * The three proposal operations a non-human actor may only perform on a
   196	 * proposal it authored itself.
   197	 */
   198	export type ProposalBindingGate =
   199	  /** `object_accepted` citing a proposal somebody else staged. */
   200	  | 'acceptance_binding'
   201	  /** `proposal_rejected` against a proposal somebody else staged. */
   202	  | 'rejection_binding'
   203	  /** `proposal_superseded` against a proposal somebody else staged. */
   204	  | 'supersession_binding';
   205	
   206	/**
   207	 * May this actor act on a proposal this proposer staged?
   208	 *
   209	 * A **human** may act on any proposal — that is the whole product: a person
   210	 * reads what the machine staged and judges it. The direction that is closed is
   211	 * the other one.
   212	 *
   213	 * A **model** may act only on its own proposals, matched by model id. Two
   214	 * interpreters run against the same room (#7's two tiers, by construction), and
   215	 * without this the cheap tier could accept the expensive tier's staged readings,
   216	 * or retire them before anyone saw them. Model id is the only identity a model
   217	 * actor carries, so it is the identity that binds.
   218	 *
   219	 * A **system** actor never matches, because `Proposer` has no system variant:
   220	 * nothing the system emits was ever staged by the system, so there is no
   221	 * proposal it can own. It may still do everything a system actor could do
   222	 * before — this closes a door that was never open.
   223	 */
   224	export function actorMatchesProposer(actor: Actor, proposer: Proposer): boolean {
   225	  if (actor.kind === 'human') return true;
   226	  if (actor.kind === 'model') return proposer.kind === 'model' && proposer.model === actor.model;
   227	  return false;
   228	}
   229	
   230	/** A short name for a proposer, for refusal texts. */
   231	function proposerName(proposer: Proposer): string {
   232	  return proposer.kind === 'model' ? `model "${proposer.model}"` : `user "${proposer.userId}"`;
   233	}
   234	
   235	/** A short name for an actor, for refusal texts. */
   236	export function actorName(actor: Actor): string {
   237	  switch (actor.kind) {
   238	    case 'human':
   239	      return `user "${actor.userId}"`;
   240	    case 'model':
   241	      return `model "${actor.model}"`;
   242	    case 'system':
   243	      return 'the system actor';
   244	    default: {
   245	      const exhaustive: never = actor;
   246	      return JSON.stringify(exhaustive);
   247	    }
   248	  }
   249	}
   250	
   251	/** The refusal text for a proposal-binding gate. Kept beside the predicate. */
   252	export function proposalBindingRefusal(
   253	  gate: ProposalBindingGate,
   254	  actor: Actor,
   255	  proposer: Proposer,
   256	  proposalId: string,
   257	): string {
   258	  const who = actorName(actor);
   259	  const whose = proposerName(proposer);
   260	  switch (gate) {
   261	    case 'acceptance_binding':
   262	      return `proposal "${proposalId}" was staged by ${whose} and ${who} tried to accept it — a non-human actor may only accept its own reading; accepting somebody else's is minting their judgement`;
   263	    case 'rejection_binding':
   264	      return `proposal "${proposalId}" was staged by ${whose} and ${who} tried to reject it — a non-human actor may only withdraw its own reading; only a human may reject another's`;
   265	    case 'supersession_binding':
   266	      return `proposal "${proposalId}" was staged by ${whose} and ${who} tried to supersede it — a non-human actor may only retire its own reading with a newer one`;
   267	    default: {
   268	      const exhaustive: never = gate;
   269	      return `unknown proposal binding gate ${JSON.stringify(exhaustive)}`;
   270	    }
   271	  }
   272	}
   273	
   274	/** The refusal text for the confidence floor. */
   275	export function confidenceFloorRefusal(
   276	  actor: Actor,
   277	  type: AcceptedObjectType,
   278	  proposalId: string,
   279	  confidence: number,
   280	): string {
   281	  const floor = MODEL_ACCEPTANCE_FLOOR[type];
   282	  return `${actorName(actor)} accepted ${type} proposal "${proposalId}" at confidence ${confidence}, below the floor of ${floor} for that type — a non-human actor may not mint an object from a reading it does not stand behind; propose it and let a human accept`;
   283	}
   284	
   285	/* ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   286	 * The receipt, as a condition of folding
   287	 * ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── */
   288	
   289	/**
   290	 * Why a non-human acceptance was refused on its receipt rather than on its
   291	 * actor. One name per check, so a caller can switch and a test can pin.
   292	 */
   293	export type AcceptanceReceiptGate =
   294	  /** The object's payload is not the payload that was staged. */
   295	  | 'payload_binding'
   296	  /** The object's cited messages are not the proposal's. */
   297	  | 'provenance_binding'
   298	  /**
   299	   * No message window — **absent, empty, or carrying no message with any content
   300	   * in it** — so the receipt cannot be checked at all. The three were separate
   301	   * checks away from each other across rounds 2 and 3 and are one check now:
   302	   * `messages: []` and `[{body: ''}]` are not windows somebody supplied, they are
   303	   * the same absence spelled differently.
   304	   */
   305	  | 'missing_receipt_context'
   306	  /**
   307	   * A machine reading that quotes nothing. Re-required here rather than trusted
   308	   * from the schema a layer up — round 2's gauntlet found that layer was never
   309	   * reached — and required for **every** model-minted type since r4, because
   310	   * round 3's gauntlet minted an objective through the hole where it was not.
   311	   */
   312	  | 'missing_quote'
   313	  /** The receipt is wrong: the quote, who is named in it, or what it says. */
   314	  | 'receipt_failed'
   315	  /**
   316	   * The receipt could not be judged either way, so a machine may not act on it.
   317	   * The quote contains every word of the statement, in order, and says more —
   318	   * and those extra words may be an aside or may be "not". A person decides.
   319	   */
   320	  | 'receipt_not_certifiable'
   321	  /** A commitment somebody else's sentence put a name on. It needs their word. */
   322	  | 'third_party_confirm';
   323	
   324	export interface AcceptanceReceiptRefusal {
   325	  gate: AcceptanceReceiptGate;
   326	  detail: string;
   327	}
   328	
   329	/**
   330	 * Everything a **non-human** acceptance must survive beyond "may this actor
   331	 * touch this proposal at all".
   332	 *
   333	 * Each of these was a call-site manner in round 1 and is a trust boundary now.
   334	 * They run in this order because the answers get more expensive and less
   335	 * certain as you go down: what was staged is a fact about two payloads, the
   336	 * receipt is a fact about the room's messages, and third-party attribution is a
   337	 * judgement about a person.
   338	 *
   339	 *  1. **Payload binding.** The proposal is the thing a person could have read
   340	 *     before it landed. An acceptance that cites it while minting a *different*
   341	 *     statement — or a different owner — has used the citation as a permission
   342	 *     slip: r1's gauntlet, "staging a self-owned commitment then minting a
   343	 *     third-party one". Canonical equality, so key order is never a difference.
   344	 *  2. **Provenance binding.** Same argument, applied to the receipt: an
   345	 *     acceptance may not add or drop cited messages on the way through, because
   346	 *     the citation set is what the attribution rules below are computed over.
   347	 *  3. **A window at all.** No messages, no auto-acceptance, ever — and `[]` is
   348	 *     no messages, and so is one message with nothing in it. Round 2's gauntlet
   349	 *     found the `undefined`-only check; round 3's found the `length`-only one.
   350	 *     Absent, empty, and empty-of-content are the same fact about the world.
   351	 *  4. **A quote at all**, for every model-minted type. The schema requires it;
   352	 *     this re-requires it, because round 2's gauntlet found the schema was never
   353	 *     run on the fold path. An empty quote is the same absence as a missing one,
   354	 *     and it is the input the bearing and attribution rules are computed from.
   355	 *  5. **The receipt itself** — the quote is long enough to identify a sentence,
   356	 *     it is in a cited message, it is that author's own text rather than
   357	 *     something they were quoting, exactly one author carries it, and the
   358	 *     statement being minted is that quote with nothing dropped but articles.
   359	 *  6. **Certifiability.** A quote that says *more* than the statement is not
   360	 *     refused as wrong and is not accepted as right: nothing here can tell an
   361	 *     aside from a "not", so a machine may not act on it and a person must.
   362	 *  7. **Third-party attribution.** A commitment whose owner did not write the
   363	 *     message bearing it is not a self-statement, and #4 is unambiguous that
   364	 *     nobody gets committed by someone else's sentence. It is a real reading and
   365	 *     it goes to the named person to confirm — through a human acceptance, not
   366	 *     through this path.
   367	 *
   368	 * A human acceptance runs none of this: a person accepting a reading has read
   369	 * it, and their judgement is the receipt. That asymmetry is the product.
   370	 */
   371	export function acceptanceReceiptRefusal(input: {
   372	  actor: Actor;
   373	  proposalId: string;
   374	  proposal: StoredProposal;
   375	  object: AcceptedObject;
   376	  messages: readonly ProvenanceMessage[] | undefined;
   377	}): AcceptanceReceiptRefusal | null {
   378	  const { object, proposal, proposalId } = input;
   379	  const who = actorName(input.actor);
   380	
   381	  if (canonicalJson(object.payload) !== canonicalJson(proposal.payload)) {
   382	    return {
   383	      gate: 'payload_binding',
   384	      detail: `object "${object.id}" cites proposal "${proposalId}" but does not carry its payload — ${who} may only accept the reading that was staged, not mint a different one behind a citation that says a person could have checked it`,
   385	    };
   386	  }
   387	
   388	  const objectCites = [...object.provenance.messageIds].sort();
   389	  const proposalCites = [...proposal.provenance].sort();
   390	  if (canonicalJson(objectCites) !== canonicalJson(proposalCites)) {
   391	    return {
   392	      gate: 'provenance_binding',
   393	      detail: `object "${object.id}" cites messages [${objectCites.join(', ')}] but proposal "${proposalId}" was staged against [${proposalCites.join(', ')}] — the receipt may not change on the way through acceptance; it is what the attribution rules are computed over`,
   394	    };
   395	  }
   396	
   397	  // ── A window at all, and emptiness as a property of the content ───────────
   398	  //
   399	  // Round 2 closed `undefined`; round 2's gauntlet reopened it with `[]`; round
   400	  // 3's gauntlet reopened it again with `[{ id, authorId, body: '' }]` — one
   401	  // message, so `length === 0` is false, and nothing in it. All three are the
   402	  // same fact: nobody supplied the messages this reading cites. The test is
   403	  // therefore about content, not about the array, and `hasContent` is the one
   404	  // place that decides what content is (a letter or a digit — not a list of the
   405	  // invisible characters somebody has thought of).
   406	  const window = input.messages;
   407	  if (window === undefined || window.length === 0 || !window.some((m) => hasContent(m.body))) {
   408	    const how =
   409	      window === undefined
   410	        ? 'no message window supplied'
   411	        : window.length === 0
   412	          ? 'an empty message window supplied'
   413	          : `a message window of ${window.length} message${window.length === 1 ? '' : 's'} with nothing in any of their bodies`;
   414	    return {
   415	      gate: 'missing_receipt_context',
   416	      detail: `${who} accepted proposal "${proposalId}" with ${how}, so its receipt could not be checked — a reading whose citation cannot be verified is refused, never accepted on trust; a window with no words in it is not a window, it is the same absence written differently`,
   417	    };
   418	  }
   419	
   420	  // ── A quote at all, for every model-minted type ───────────────────────────
   421	  //
   422	  // Scoped to claims and commitments until r4, on the argument that they are the
   423	  // two that put a name on somebody. That is true and it is not the whole job:
   424	  // r3's gauntlet minted a model *objective* with `quote: null` against a window
   425	  // of empty bodies, and no quote-length, bearing or attribution check ran on it
   426	  // at all. The quote is what answers "which sentence, in which message" — every
   427	  // type needs that answer, and a type with no quote has no receipt to check.
   428	  if (isBlank(proposal.quote)) {
   429	    return {
   430	      gate: 'missing_quote',
   431	      detail: `${who} accepted ${object.type} proposal "${proposalId}", which quotes nothing — a receipt names the sentence it rests on and only the quote identifies it, so a model-minted ${object.type} with an absent or empty quote is refused rather than resting on whoever happens to be in the citation list`,
   432	    };
   433	  }
   434	
   435	  const attributedTo =
   436	    object.type === 'claim'
   437	      ? object.payload.claimant
   438	      : object.type === 'commitment'
   439	        ? object.payload.owner
   440	        : null;
   441	
   442	  const problems = validateProposalProvenance(
   443	    {
   444	      type: proposal.type,
   445	      provenance: proposal.provenance,
   446	      quote: proposal.quote,
   447	      proposer: proposal.proposer,
   448	      attributedTo,
   449	      // The sentence the object actually asserts — checked *from the object*
   450	      // rather than from the proposal on purpose. Payload binding above has
   451	      // already proved the two are identical, so reading it here says what this
   452	      // check is about: the quote has to bear the thing being minted.
   453	      statement: objectStatement(object),
   454	    },
   455	    window,
   456	  );
   457	
   458	  const rejecting = rejectingProblems(problems);
   459	  if (rejecting.length > 0) {
   460	    return {
   461	      gate: 'receipt_failed',
   462	      detail: `${who} accepted proposal "${proposalId}" on a receipt that does not hold: ${rejecting.map((problem) => problem.detail).join('; ')}`,
   463	    };
   464	  }
   465	
   466	  // A receipt the check declines to rule on is not a receipt that passed. The
   467	  // acceptance is refused with its own gate name so the log distinguishes "the
   468	  // citation is wrong" from "the citation may be right and nothing here can say
   469	  // so" — the second one is a question for a person, and a person accepting it
   470	  // runs none of this.
   471	  const referring = referringProblems(problems);
   472	  if (referring.length > 0) {
   473	    return {
   474	      gate: 'receipt_not_certifiable',
   475	      detail: `${who} accepted proposal "${proposalId}" on a receipt this check declines to rule on: ${referring.map((problem) => problem.detail).join('; ')}`,
   476	    };
   477	  }
   478	
   479	  const thirdParty = problems.find((problem) => problem.kind === 'attributed_person_not_author');
   480	  if (thirdParty) {
   481	    return {
   482	      gate: 'third_party_confirm',
   483	      detail: `${who} accepted proposal "${proposalId}" as a commitment for somebody who did not write it: ${thirdParty.detail}. Nobody gets committed by someone else's sentence (#4) — it waits for the named owner to confirm, and only a human acceptance can carry that confirmation`,
   484	    };
   485	  }
   486	
   487	  return null;
   488	}
   650	function applyObjectAccepted(
   651	  state: CoreState,
   652	  event: EventOf<'object_accepted'>,
   653	  actor: Actor,
   654	  messages: TrustedContext['messages'],
   655	): boolean {
   656	  const { object } = event;
   657	  if (state.objects[object.id]) {
   658	    fail(state, event.id, `object "${object.id}" already accepted`);
   659	    return false;
   660	  }
   661	
   662	  // ── The actor floor (see `authority.ts` for #4's matrix) ──
   663	  //
   664	  // Checked before the proposal citation, because authority is a property of
   665	  // the event and citation is a property of what it points at: a model that may
   666	  // not accept this object at all should be told that, not sent to fix its
   667	  // provenance first.
   668	  const subject = `object "${object.id}"`;
   669	
   670	  // The types a machine may not mint at all, whatever its confidence.
   671	  //
   672	  // A decision is the one #4 bans inference at by name: "that sounds good" is
   673	  // where the ambiguity lives. r5 adds commitment and objective — see
   674	  // `modelMintingGate`, which reads the same θ table `MODEL_ACCEPTANCE_FLOOR`
   675	  // does, so this gate and that floor cannot disagree about which types are in
   676	  // the row. Note this gate does not care whether a proposal was cited — an
   677	  // interpreter accepting *its own* decision proposal is exactly the move.
   678	  const mintingGate = modelMintingGate(object.type);
   679	  if (mintingGate !== null && !isHuman(actor)) {
   680	    fail(state, event.id, humanOnlyRefusal(mintingGate, actor, subject));
   681	    return false;
   682	  }
   683	
   684	  // `~` vs `✓`, as data. A claim may be model-accepted — that is #4's
   685	  // auto-accept path and it stays open — but it arrives unverified. Verified is
   686	  // the room asserting the claim is true, and nothing model-accepted ever
   687	  // renders as fact.
   688	  if (object.type === 'claim' && object.payload.verification === 'verified' && !isHuman(actor)) {
   689	    fail(state, event.id, humanOnlyRefusal('claim_verification', actor, subject));
   690	    return false;
   691	  }
   692	
   693	  // An object may be born without a proposal — but only from a human. That is
   694	  // the answer-binding path: a person writes a decision, or binds an answer,
   695	  // and a person's word is not an interpretation that needs accepting.
   696	  //
   697	  // A model or a system actor has exactly one way to mint a fact: propose it,
   698	  // and have the proposal accepted. Without this gate the whole acceptance
   699	  // boundary is optional — an interpreter that cannot hand itself a
   700	  // pre-accepted proposal can simply skip the proposal and emit the object.
   701	  const proposalId = object.provenance.proposalId;
   702	  if (proposalId === null && !isHuman(actor)) {
   703	    fail(state, event.id, humanOnlyRefusal('direct_acceptance', actor, subject));
   704	    return false;
   705	  }
   706	
   707	  // An object that *claims* a proposal must claim a real, still-open,
   708	  // type-matching one: that citation is the provenance the UI shows, so an
   709	  // unverifiable one is worse than none.
   710	  let proposal: ProposalRecord | undefined;
   711	  if (proposalId !== null) {
   712	    proposal = state.proposals[proposalId];
   713	    if (!proposal) {
   714	      fail(state, event.id, `object "${object.id}" cites unknown proposal "${proposalId}"`);
   715	      return false;
   716	    }
   717	    if (proposal.status === 'rejected') {
   718	      fail(state, event.id, `proposal "${proposalId}" was already rejected`);
   719	      return false;
   720	    }
   721	    if (proposal.status === 'accepted' || proposal.acceptedObjectId !== null) {
   722	      fail(
   723	        state,
   724	        event.id,
   725	        `proposal "${proposalId}" was already accepted as object "${proposal.acceptedObjectId}"`,
   726	      );
   727	      return false;
   728	    }
   729	    if (proposal.status === 'superseded') {
   730	      fail(
   731	        state,
   732	        event.id,
   733	        `proposal "${proposalId}" was already superseded by "${proposal.supersededByProposalId ?? 'a newer reading'}" — accept the reading that replaced it`,
   734	      );
   735	      return false;
   736	    }
   737	
   738	    // A non-human actor may only accept what it itself staged. Two interpreters
   739	    // run against one room by construction (#7's two tiers), and without this
   740	    // the cheap tier can land the expensive tier's staged readings — or a
   741	    // person's, which is a machine minting a human's judgement.
   742	    if (!actorMatchesProposer(actor, proposal.proposal.proposer)) {
   743	      fail(
   744	        state,
   745	        event.id,
   746	        proposalBindingRefusal('acceptance_binding', actor, proposal.proposal.proposer, proposalId),
   747	      );
   748	      return false;
   749	    }
   750	
   751	    if (proposal.proposal.type !== object.type) {
   752	      fail(
   753	        state,
   754	        event.id,
   755	        `proposal "${proposalId}" is a ${proposal.proposal.type}, cannot be accepted as a ${object.type}`,
   756	      );
   757	      return false;
   758	    }
   759	    if (proposal.proposal.roomId !== object.roomId) {
   760	      fail(
   761	        state,
   762	        event.id,
   763	        `proposal "${proposalId}" belongs to room "${proposal.proposal.roomId}", object "${object.id}" to room "${object.roomId}"`,
   764	      );
   765	      return false;
   766	    }
   767	
   768	    // ── θ, as a trust boundary rather than a manner ──
   769	    //
   770	    // The gates above close the *types* a model may not mint. This closes the
   771	    // rest of the write surface, and it is the engine's own θ_auto: round 1's
   772	    // gauntlet routed the split (a separate, lower "malformed" floor) as a hole,
   773	    // because a model could land a claim at 0.55 that the engine at 0.7 would
   774	    // never have emitted. One number, read from `policy.ts` by both.
   775	    if (!isHuman(actor)) {
   776	      // ── The receipt, and it is ruled on before the confidence ──
   777	      //
   778	      // Payload binding, provenance binding, a message window at all, the quote,
   779	      // and third-party attribution. All five were call-site manners in round 1.
   780	      //
   781	      // **Above the confidence floor since r5**, for the reason `acceptance.ts`
   782	      // gives at length where the same ordering mattered more: the two answer
   783	      // different questions. The floor asks how sure the model was; the receipt
   784	      // asks whether the citation and the sentence agree. Both refuse the fold,
   785	      // so nothing is at stake here except which reason the room is shown — and
   786	      // "the quote says the opposite of the statement" is the one worth showing.
   787	      // A refusal that reports the cheaper finding buries the more serious one.
   788	      const refusal = acceptanceReceiptRefusal({
   789	        actor,
   790	        proposalId,
   791	        proposal: proposal.proposal,
   792	        object,
   793	        messages,
   794	      });
   795	      if (refusal) {
   796	        fail(state, event.id, refusal.detail);
   797	        return false;
   798	      }
   799	
   800	      // ── …and θ, as a trust boundary rather than a manner ──
   801	      const floor = MODEL_ACCEPTANCE_FLOOR[object.type];
   802	      if (proposal.proposal.confidence < floor) {
   803	        fail(
   804	          state,
   805	          event.id,
   806	          confidenceFloorRefusal(actor, object.type, proposalId, proposal.proposal.confidence),
   807	        );
   808	        return false;
   809	      }
   810	    }
   811	  }
   812	
   813	  // An `objectiveId` that points at nothing is the same defect as a `proposalId`
   814	  // that points at nothing, and is refused for the same reason: it is provenance
   815	  // the UI renders, and an unverifiable one is worse than none. The object files
   816	  // itself under a heading the room cannot open, which reads as "this belongs to
   817	  // something" while belonging to nothing.
   818	  //
   819	  // Refused rather than recorded-and-applied, because `reduce` sorts: in a
   820	  // replay the objective would already be there if it ever existed, so a dangling
   821	  // pointer is not a race, it is wrong. The acceptance can be re-minted with a
   822	  // corrected pointer; a fact filed under a ghost cannot be un-filed.
   823	  const objectiveId = object.objectiveId;
   824	  if (objectiveId !== null) {
   825	    const objective = state.objects[objectiveId];
   826	    if (!objective) {
   827	      fail(
   828	        state,
   829	        event.id,
   830	        `object "${object.id}" belongs to objective "${objectiveId}", which does not exist`,
   831	      );
   832	      return false;
   833	    }
   834	    if (objective.object.type !== 'objective') {
   835	      fail(
   836	        state,
   837	        event.id,
   838	        `object "${object.id}" belongs to "${objectiveId}", which is a ${objective.object.type}, not an objective`,
   839	      );
   840	      return false;
   841	    }
   842	    if (objective.object.roomId !== object.roomId) {
   843	      fail(
   844	        state,
   845	        event.id,
   846	        `object "${object.id}" is in room "${object.roomId}" but its objective "${objectiveId}" is in room "${objective.object.roomId}"`,
   847	      );
   848	      return false;
   849	    }
   850	  }
   851	
   852	  const record: ObjectRecord = {
   853	    object,
   854	    acceptedAt: event.at,
   855	    updatedAt: event.at,
   856	    // `~` vs `✓` is derived from this and nothing else — see `epistemic.ts`.
   857	    acceptedBy: actor,
   858	    humanTouchedAt: isHuman(actor) ? event.at : null,
   859	    revision: 0,
   860	    retractedAt: null,
   861	    supersededById: null,
   862	    reopenedFromAnswers: [],
   863	  };
   864	  state.objects[object.id] = record;
   865	
   866	  if (proposal) {
   867	    proposal.status = 'accepted';
   868	    proposal.acceptedObjectId = object.id;
   869	  }
   870	  return true;
   871	}
   872	
   873	function applyObjectCorrected(
   874	  state: CoreState,
   875	  event: EventOf<'object_corrected'>,
   876	  actor: Actor,
   877	): boolean {
   878	  const record = state.objects[event.objectId];
   879	  if (!record) {
   880	    fail(state, event.id, `unknown object "${event.objectId}"`);
   881	    return false;
   882	  }
   883	
   884	  // Every correction verb is human-only in v1 (#4, and the correction verbs
   885	  // #21 grew are the same shape). A correction rewrites, withdraws or restores
   886	  // something the room already accepted — it is the one operation that reaches
   887	  // backwards, and an interpreter must never reach backwards on its own
   888	  // authority. A model that thinks an object is wrong proposes a supersession.
   889	  //
   890	  // The claim-verification gate is repeated here rather than left to the
   891	  // blanket rule above it. Today it is unreachable — a model cannot amend
   892	  // anything — but the two rules have different lifetimes: a later round may
   893	  // hand a model some correction verb, and "a claim only becomes ✓ by a human"
   894	  // must not depend on that never happening.
   895	  if (!isHuman(actor)) {
   896	    const verifying =
   897	      event.patch.verification === 'verified' &&
   898	      (record.object.type === 'claim' || event.toType === 'claim');
   899	    fail(
   900	      state,
   901	      event.id,
   902	      humanOnlyRefusal(
   903	        verifying ? 'claim_verification' : 'correction',
   904	        actor,
   905	        `object "${event.objectId}"`,
   906	      ),
   907	    );
   908	    return false;
   909	  }
   910	
   911	  if (event.action === 'retract') {
   912	    if (record.retractedAt !== null) {
   913	      fail(state, event.id, `object "${event.objectId}" is already retracted`);
   914	      return false;
   915	    }
   916	    commitCorrection(state, event, record, actor, { retracted: false }, { retracted: true });
   917	    record.retractedAt = event.at;
   918	    return true;
   919	  }
   920	
   921	  if (event.action === 'restore') {
   922	    if (record.retractedAt === null) {
   923	      fail(state, event.id, `object "${event.objectId}" is not retracted`);
   924	      return false;
   925	    }
   926	    commitCorrection(state, event, record, actor, { retracted: true }, { retracted: false });
   927	    record.retractedAt = null;
   928	    return true;
   929	  }
   930	
   931	  // A retracted object is withdrawn, not editable. Editing one would quietly
   932	  // resurrect content the room already took back; restore it first, in the open.
   933	  if (record.retractedAt !== null) {
   934	    fail(
   935	      state,
   936	      event.id,
   937	      `object "${event.objectId}" is retracted — restore it before ${event.action === 'amend' ? 'amending' : `applying "${event.action}"`}`,
   938	    );
   939	    return false;
   940	  }

   330	
   331	const declined = (undecidable: StatementBearing['undecidable']): StatementBearing => ({
   332	  borne: false,
   333	  unmatchedInStatement: [],
   334	  unmatchedInQuote: [],
   335	  undecidable,
   336	});
   337	
   338	/**
   339	 * The alignment itself, over tokens somebody else produced.
   340	 *
   341	 * Split out from `statementBearing` in r5 so the later-correction detector can
   342	 * run the same comparison over *routing* tokens without a second implementation
   343	 * of the alignment. One alignment, two tokenizations, and the caller says which
   344	 * — rather than two alignments that can drift.
   345	 *
   346	 * Greedy, with a one-sided lookahead so a single extra word resynchronises
   347	 * instead of scrambling the rest of the comparison. Deterministic, total, and
   348	 * bounded by `RECEIPT_POLICY.maxAlignedTokens` — an input too big to align is
   349	 * refused, not approximated.
   350	 */
   351	export function alignTokens(
   352	  q: readonly string[],
   353	  s: readonly string[],
   354	  policy: ReceiptPolicy = RECEIPT_POLICY,
   355	): StatementBearing {
   356	  if (q.length === 0) return declined('empty_quote');
   357	  if (s.length === 0) return declined('empty_statement');
   358	  if (q.length > policy.maxAlignedTokens || s.length > policy.maxAlignedTokens) {
   359	    return declined('too_long');
   360	  }
   361	
   362	  const droppable = (token: string | undefined): boolean =>
   363	    token !== undefined && policy.droppableTokens.has(token);
   364	
   365	  const unmatchedInQuote: string[] = [];
   366	  const unmatchedInStatement: string[] = [];
   367	  let i = 0;
   368	  let j = 0;
   369	
   370	  while (i < q.length && j < s.length) {
   371	    const qt = q[i];
   372	    const st = s[j];
   373	    if (qt === st) {
   374	      i += 1;
   375	      j += 1;
   376	      continue;
   377	    }
   378	    // A full stop on either side may be skipped silently — that is the entire
   379	    // licence this check grants, and `droppableTokens` says why.
   380	    if (droppable(qt)) {
   381	      i += 1;
   382	      continue;
   383	    }
   384	    if (droppable(st)) {
   385	      j += 1;
   386	      continue;
   387	    }
   388	    // A real disagreement. Resynchronise towards whichever side can still be
   389	    // matched, so one interposed word ("not") is reported as one interposed word
   390	    // rather than knocking every later token out of alignment.
   391	    if (st !== undefined && q.indexOf(st, i + 1) !== -1) {
   392	      if (qt !== undefined) unmatchedInQuote.push(qt);
   393	      i += 1;
   394	    } else {
   395	      if (st !== undefined) unmatchedInStatement.push(st);
   396	      j += 1;
   397	    }
   398	  }
   399	
   400	  // Whatever is left over on either side is unaccounted for — a trailing
   401	  // "unless CI is red" is exactly this case, and it is why the tail is not
   402	  // forgiven.
   403	  for (; i < q.length; i += 1) {
   404	    const token = q[i];
   405	    if (!droppable(token) && token !== undefined) unmatchedInQuote.push(token);
   406	  }
   407	  for (; j < s.length; j += 1) {
   408	    const token = s[j];
   409	    if (!droppable(token) && token !== undefined) unmatchedInStatement.push(token);
   410	  }
   411	
   412	  return {
   413	    borne: unmatchedInQuote.length === 0 && unmatchedInStatement.length === 0,
   414	    unmatchedInStatement,
   415	    unmatchedInQuote,
   416	    undecidable: null,
   417	  };
   418	}
   419
